The replacement domain initially had little to no reputation-based detection while the phishing chain remained active.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy 2026-06-10, that had started to change. Fortinet marked the domain as phishing and ESET flagged it as suspicious, but the operator had already been using it as failover infrastructure after coldchain-distribution[.]com was burned.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat Mailer-Go is\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go Mission Control presents itself like a commercial email campaign platform. Its admin panel includes modules for campaign management, audience lists, email creation, SMTP providers, proxy routing, tracking, licensing and update notifications.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNone of those features, viewed individually, proves malicious intent. The combination is harder to explain as a normal bulk-mail product.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go supports proxy routing and multiple SMTP providers, and it generates single-use tracking tokens that stop exposing the live destination after the first click. It also includes built-in license management, yet at the time of our analysis we could find no public documentation, GitHub footprint or visible legitimate product presence. The same Mailer-Go login page was exposed across wildcard campaign subdomains.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe assess with moderate confidence that Mailer-Go is purpose-built crimeware rather than a legitimate phishing simulation tool being abused. That assessment is based on the platform as a whole: proxy support, flexible sending infrastructure, token burning, controlled access and the absence of a public legitimate footprint.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUseful fingerprints include:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go Mission Control\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/js/tracking.js?v=20260522d\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/js/proxies.js?v=20260513b\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/js/sending-infrastructure.js?v=20260513b\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/dist/emailbuilder2/manifest.json\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe observed admin panel HTML had this SHA-256:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e3a026fcb5001327198ac3886e6983183e44b8a5daa0086d34c10cb65130c6f89\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLive tokens showed the real destination\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe expired links were only part of the picture. URLScan captures of tokens that were still live showed a different path.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eValid Mailer-Go tracking links routed through:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003emysocalledblog[.]com/sev/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat relay then redirected the victim to a Cloudflare Workers page. Following the EvilTokens convention, the Workers subdomain encoded the targeted recipient's email address, which we have redacted:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e9sfo-bnqi-4yap.[recipient-redacted]-s-account.workers[.]dev\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe page presented itself as a OneDrive shared-document flow built around a file named Confidential_Memo.docx. Captures showed several stages of the page, including a “Preparing verification...” screen, a transient “fetch failed” error and, most importantly, the page displaying a Microsoft device code.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=EiTv6k-l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=kqQcOUui 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":878},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=LPQk8dae 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=kLQ0DqWo 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=xEnob1Sc 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=A8Jt_Ax4 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 2: The final page displays a Microsoft device code and instructs the victim to continue to Microsoft.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=xEnob1Sc","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThere was no fake Microsoft password form. The victim was sent to the real Microsoft device login page. If they entered the code and completed authentication, the attacker-controlled client could receive OAuth tokens in the background.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat has an important consequence for incident response. Resetting the user's password may not be enough if a refresh token has already been issued. We cover the response steps later in the defenders section.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhy Visa appeared in the redirect chain\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eExpired tokens followed another route.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstead of reaching the EvilTokens page, they passed through:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003earduinosolutions[.]com/sev/\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eand then landed on legitimate Visa sites such as visa[.]fr, visa[.]com, visa[.]de and others.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat initially creates a confusing picture because Visa appears repeatedly in the redirect data, yet the confirmed phishing lure impersonated Microsoft OneDrive.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe explanation was simpler. Visa acted as fallback cover for dead tokens. Once a tracking token had expired, the user was sent to a legitimate site rather than the phishing page. Someone clicking the link later could reasonably conclude that nothing malicious had happened, and an analyst reconstructing the chain after the fact could reach the same wrong conclusion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe emails were built to survive static scanning\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe identified five phishing EML samples tied to the campaign. Their MIME-header similarity supports the conclusion that they came from the same sending platform.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe messages used generic B2B document themes, including an Autodesk-themed “Bidding Addendum” lure. We found no evidence of detailed industry reconnaissance, so the activity looks more like broad B2B targeting than a tightly tailored campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe email bodies also used a simple obfuscation technique in which characters were interleaved with question marks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eT?h?i?s?\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe text remains readable to a person, but the added characters interfere with keyword matching and some content-based detection systems. At the time of analysis, the observed samples had zero detections across the engines available in our internal dataset.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also saw Microsoft SafeLinks wrapping in some of the messages. In those cases, the original Mailer-Go URL was stored in the originalsrc attribute rather than the visible href, so analysts reviewing mail traces should inspect both.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat defenders should take from this\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most useful Mailer-Go indicators are likely to come from the delivery layer rather than the lure itself. Phishing content can be changed quickly, and a Workers URL can disappear. The tracking structure is more consistent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this campaign, Mailer-Go links used *.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt; subdomains and a /t/v3. path containing a numeric campaign ID followed by a long hexadecimal token:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://\u0026lt;8-hex\u0026gt;.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt;/t/v3.\u0026lt;campaign-id\u0026gt;.\u0026lt;recipient-token\u0026gt;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe admin panel also exposed reusable fingerprints, including the title Mailer-Go Mission Control and versioned JavaScript paths such as:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e/assets/js/tracking.js?v=20260522d\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e/assets/js/proxies.js?v=20260513b\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e/assets/dist/emailbuilder2/manifest.json\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor Microsoft 365 environments, a confirmed device-code phishing event should not be handled as ordinary password phishing. If the attacker received a refresh token, resetting the password may leave access intact. Response should include revoking active sessions and refresh tokens, reviewing device-code sign-ins, checking app consent, reviewing registered authentication methods and devices, and looking for new mailbox rules, MFA changes or suspicious file access after the click.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttribution and limits\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe have not linked this campaign to a named threat actor.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operator appears to be a customer of EvilTokens, which is commercially sold. Mailer-Go is less clear. Its licensing features and commercial-style architecture suggest controlled distribution, but we do not know how the platform is sold or shared, and we cannot rule out the possibility that the operator behind this campaign is also involved in its development.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are other limits to what the available evidence can tell us. We observed a low-confidence PE download signal in sandbox telemetry, but no executable was recovered and the same signal appeared when sandboxing the Mailer-Go admin panel itself. Without endpoint evidence showing a PE download or execution after a victim click, we do not treat that as confirmed victim-side malware delivery.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eConclusion\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe reported link was already burned, but it still exposed the campaign’s delivery architecture. Behind the expired URL sat a Mailer-Go tracking server, a backup domain with a fake root site, relay nodes of unconfirmed ownership and an EvilTokens page abusing Microsoft’s device-code flow.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go is the part defenders should track beyond this campaign. The final lure can change. The Workers URL can disappear. The delivery layer leaves stronger patterns: *.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt; subdomains, /t/v3 tracking paths, single-use tokens, exposed admin panels and a small set of reusable JS fingerprints.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSources and credits\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e85ca59061a0c2a3192dac54b484f8035\"\u003eWebsite snapshots (Figures fake invoice in your email is easy to ignore. A fake invoice inside your order history feels different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNorton customers have reported fake Norton invoices appearing inside the Shop app, the shopping and order-tracking app from Shopify. Public reports suggest the same technique is not limited to Norton. Similar suspicious Shop app notifications have used McAfee, Apple gift cards, iPhones, PayPal-style payment claims and other high-value purchases as bait. The impersonated brand may change, but the mechanics are familiar: make the user believe they have been charged, then give them a phone number to call.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat phone number is where the scam really begins.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy the Shop app makes this more believable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eShop is a legitimate app. People use it to track packages, check receipts and receive order updates. According to Shop’s own documentation, the app can automatically track orders from several sources, including Gmail, Outlook, the email address linked to the user’s Shop account and orders paid through Shop Pay. If Gmail or Outlook is connected, Shop says it scans recent emails for keywords such as “tracking number” and “track your package” to populate deliveries in the Orders tab.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=Bsgcxhe6 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=RisRR8-Z 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":1170},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=eSy-7wue 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=3rbMO4E_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":890},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=1cSllT8s 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=DxHsElW1 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":690}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=1cSllT8s","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eShop also supports push notifications for delivery updates, payments and favorite products. So the first alert the victim sees may not be an email, an SMS or a calendar invite. It may be a notification from an app they already use for real purchases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat context matters. The scam text is not especially polished. Some of the examples we reviewed contain awkward wording and obvious signs of fraud. But when the message appears inside an order-tracking app, surrounded by real receipts and shipping updates, users may give it more credibility than it deserves.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe screenshots reported to us show fake orders under generic seller names such as “My Store.” The receipts claim that a Norton or Norton LifeLock subscription has been activated or renewed, usually for several hundred dollars. The phone number appears in the product description, the receipt body or even the shipping address field.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is not how a normal receipt should look.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe brand is interchangeable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eNorton is the example we saw through customer reports. Norton support now warns that scammers may abuse Android and iOS shopping platforms or applications to generate fake order notifications that appear to be legitimate Norton purchases. Those notifications may include fake order confirmations, fake charges, trusted third-party app delivery and fake support phone numbers embedded in order details.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePublic user reports point to a wider pattern. One Reddit user described receiving a Shop.app message about a transaction they did not recognize, with no follow-up email confirmation, and a message telling them to call support if the order was not placed by them. Another thread includes reports of unrecognized iPhone purchases under “My Store,” suspicious contact emails and users saying they saw no matching charges on their bank cards. Other reports mention fictitious Apple gift card purchases and iPhone orders using the same kind of wording: “If Order Not Placed By You,” followed by a phone number.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe choice of brand is part of the social engineering. A fake security subscription charge plays on two fears at once: being billed for something you did not buy, and being left unprotected if you ignore it. Apple products and gift cards create high-value purchase panic. PayPal-style wording makes users worry about payment account abuse. In each case, the fake order is there to push the victim toward a call.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat we know, and what we do not\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe have not found any evidence that any of the impersonated brands have been compromised.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also should not claim that Shop or Shopify was breached based on the evidence we have. The exact abuse path still needs confirmation. The fake order may be entering the app through a merchant/order workflow, through email parsing, through account association, or through another legitimate mechanism that scammers have found a way to misuse. Shop’s own documentation shows that orders can populate from multiple sources, so assuming one route would be premature.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat we can say is narrower and stronger: scammers are placing fake purchase claims inside shopping app order experiences, where users are used to seeing real receipts and order updates. Norton’s own support page now describes this class of shopping app scam, and public reports show similar lures using other brands and products.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is enough to warn users without overstating the technical evidence.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eHow the scam works\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe user receives a notification or sees a new order in the Shop app. The order looks like a receipt for something expensive: a security subscription, an Apple gift card, an iPhone, a MacBook or another high-value purchase.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/HowScamWorks.png.webp?itok=pSNPg2B3 receipt says the charge has been processed, the subscription has renewed, or the order is being prepared. Somewhere in the order details, the scammer inserts a phone number, email address or support instruction.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe wording is often clumsy. We have seen phrases like “If Order Not Place By You” and “If Need Help.” Those errors may look obvious when reviewing a screenshot. They are easier to miss when a phone notification tells you that hundreds of dollars have just been charged.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the victim calls, the scam moves off-platform. The person answering may claim to be billing support, Norton support, PayPal support or a cancellation department. The script can vary, but the request usually moves toward credentials, payment data, one-time codes or remote access software.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy that point, the fake receipt has done its job. It has created enough urgency for the victim to enter a conversation controlled by the scammer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy this is harder for users to judge\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost people understand that email can be spam. They may still fall for phishing, but at least the inbox is a place where scams are expected.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrder-tracking apps are different. Their purpose is to collect receipts, shipping updates and purchase information in one place. A notification from that environment can feel as if it has already passed through some layer of trust, even when the content inside the order is fraudulent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe have seen the same logic with calendar invite scams. The message itself is not always convincing, but the delivery channel changes how people read it. A fake invoice in an inbox is one thing. A fake invoice inside a calendar reminder or shopping app receipt can feel closer to a real event.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor security products, this also creates a detection problem. The first visible alert may not be an email, SMS or malicious website. It may be a legitimate app notification carrying fraudulent text. The scam still depends on social engineering, but the delivery surface is harder to classify with the usual rules.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you see one\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eDo not call the number in the receipt.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCheck the charge directly. Open your bank or credit card app. Open the official account for the brand being impersonated. If it claims to be Norton, go to your Norton account or official Norton support. If it claims to involve PayPal, open PayPal directly. Do not use the phone number, email address or link inside the suspicious order.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf there is no matching charge, treat the receipt as a scam attempt.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReport the suspicious store or message in the Shop app if the option is available. Shop’s help pages say suspicious Shop stores can be reported from the store page through the app, and Shopify also tells users to forward phishing messages to its phishing inbox.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNorton users can forward suspicious messages to Norton and should verify unexpected charges only through their Norton account or official Norton support channels.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOpening the receipt does not automatically mean your device was compromised. The risk increases if you call the number, share information, install software or follow instructions from the person on the phone.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you have already called\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eEnd the call.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared payment details, contact your bank or card provider immediately using the number on your card or inside your banking app. If you gave away a password, change it from a different device if possible. Start with your email account, Apple ID or Google account, banking account and any account connected to the fake invoice.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you installed remote access software, disconnect the device from the internet and remove the software. Run a security scan before using the device again for banking, email or shopping.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you gave a one-time code to the caller, treat the related account as compromised. Change the password, review recovery options and check recent login activity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe lesson\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers keep testing new places to put old tricks. For years, fake invoices were mostly an email problem. Then similar subscription notices appeared in calendar invites. Now we are seeing fake receipts inside shopping app order histories and push notification flows.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest response is to separate the alert from the action. If an app, email, text message or calendar invite says you have been charged, do not use the contact details inside that alert. Go directly to the account or payment provider yourself. Check whether the charge exists. Use official support channels.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake receipt, the fake support number and the fake emergency are all part of the same controlled environment. The moment you verify the charge somewhere else, the scam loses most of its are constantly evolving, and so are the techniques they use to bypass \u003ca href=\"https://security.googleblog.com/2024/07/improving-security-of-chrome-cookies-on.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eApplication-Bound Encryption (ABE)\u003c/a\u003e. In recent weeks, Vidar has been among the most actively developed stealers and, apart from \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2064836539952361976\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003emultiple updates to its string obfuscation\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2061462524604649840\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ea reworked approach to protecting its configuration\u003c/a\u003e, it has also introduced a novel technique for bypassing ABE. And while there have been many other changes in Vidar lately, with new versions dropping every week, in this blog post we focus solely on the ABE bypass and its technical aspects.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ABE Bypass\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Vidar’s approach to bypassing ABE is somewhat similar to that of Remus/Lumma, in that both try to extract the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly from the browser’s memory. What sets them apart, however, is how they achieve it. Since we’ve already covered the inner workings of ABE in earlier posts (\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eVoidStealer\u003c/a\u003e), we don’t revisit them here and focus instead on the bypass itself. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, the end goal of infostealers is to obtain the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, as it alone is sufficient for decrypting any ABE-protected data tied to a specific application. This key can be obtained in one of two ways: either from disk, where it is protected by two layers of \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectData\u003c/code\u003e (one applied in the context of the logged-in user and one as \u003ccode\u003eNT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM\u003c/code\u003e), or from the browser’s memory – the direction Vidar took.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn memory, however, the key is kept protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. This ensures that even if an attacker manages to read the encrypted form of the key from memory, they cannot simply decrypt it (they would need to invoke the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e function directly from within the browser process). That leaves attackers with two problems to solve: how to find the encrypted key in memory, and how to get it decrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLocating the v20_master_key in Browser’s Memory\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eVidar’s approach to locating the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in memory involves scanning the entire memory of a running browser for a specific pattern. But that first requires a browser process to scan.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the target browser is already running, Vidar reuses the existing process. However, it doesn’t read its memory directly. Instead, it creates a fork of it using \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003eSectionHandle\u003c/code\u003e set to \u003ccode\u003eNULL\u003c/code\u003e, instructing the kernel to clone the address space of the process specified by the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e as a copy-on-write mapping. The resulting process has no threads and is never resumed – it exists purely as a static memory snapshot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo perform the fork, Vidar first opens a handle to the target browser via \u003ccode\u003eOpenProcess\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS | PROCESS_VM_READ\u003c/code\u003e, falling back to \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e if the initial call fails. It then passes this handle as the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e argument to \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e, requesting \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_ALL_ACCESS\u003c/code\u003e on the resulting child. If the fork fails, Vidar falls back to scanning the live browser process directly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=a0E1a5xW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=JbjnwDnR 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":303},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=DB6yookn 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=1HwyA59k 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":230},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=8YI9qDxS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=BSGKHXK_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":179}],"caption":"Figure 1: Vidar forking a browser process. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=8YI9qDxS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIf no target browser process is found running, Vidar creates a new isolated desktop via \u003ccode\u003eCreateDesktopA\u003c/code\u003e, assigns it a name of the form \u003ccode\u003ev20_%d\u003c/code\u003e (where \u003ccode\u003e%d\u003c/code\u003e is replaced with the browser’s index in Vidar’s configuration), and launches the target browser in there. In the case of Vidar version 2.1, the browser is launched with the command line \u003ccode\u003e--no-first-run --disable-gpu about:blank\u003c/code\u003e, although the exact command line varies across versions. Since modern browsers use a multi-process architecture, there are typically multiple processes matching a given browser. Vidar enumerates all of them (up to 64) and applies the fork-and-scan procedure to each one independently.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=AJrFaBHE 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=5LsKVVrj 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":547},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=4rj4cTpo 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=qfC2N17D 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":416},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=Q_EiFHL9 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=tYP-atq_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":322}],"caption":"Figure 2: Vidar launching a browser in a new desktop. To read it, Vidar creates a second fork of the browser process and reads the key from the same address using \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e. It then compares the result against a snapshot of the encrypted key taken from the first fork before the APC call – if the data is unchanged, decryption failed, and Vidar moves on to the next browser process’s candidate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the data differs, Vidar uses the same second fork to perform an additional verification: it scans the forked process’s memory for the byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e and, treating each match as a potentially ABE-encrypted entry (cookie, password), attempts AES-256-GCM decryption with the key candidate using \u003ccode\u003eBCryptDecrypt\u003c/code\u003e (successful GCM tag verification confirms that the key is correct).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhenever the data has changed (indicating that decryption of the key took place), Vidar re-encrypts the key in the browser’s memory by injecting another APC with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e as the routine, preserving the browser’s state. If no candidate produced a successful decryption (i.e., the data remained unchanged after every APC call), Vidar takes a more aggressive approach: it terminates all browser instances, restarts the browser, and repeats the entire scan-decrypt-verify cycle.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy abusing APC injections instead of the more traditional approach of injecting code via \u003ccode\u003eNtWriteVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e that performs the decryption, Vidar trades one suspicious behavior for another. APC injections are arguably less common and may thus slip past some detection systems, though they are still well-known techniques monitored by AV and EDR solutions. But that is the nature of ABE bypasses – each has its own advantages and disadvantages, and that’s exactly why infostealers keep inventing new ones. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicator of Compromise (IoC)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e459daa809751e73f60fbbe4384a7d1653c36bb06945e4eb3635270924241100a (Vidar have a summer flight booked. You have seen the headlines about jet fuel shortages, cancelled routes and higher travel costs. Then a text message arrives: your flight has been affected, and you need to click a link to confirm your refund or rebook before the offer expires.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat message feels plausible because the situation around it is real. In April, \u003ca href=\"https://www.iata.org/en/pressroom/2026-releases/2026-04-17-01/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eIATA warned\u003c/a\u003e that Europe could start seeing flight cancellations by the end of May if jet fuel shortages materialized. Airlines and governments have since been trying to reassure travelers, while also publishing guidance on passenger rights, refunds and re-routing.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly the kind of confusion scammers like. They do not need to cause the disruption, they only need to arrive at the right moment, with the right brand logo and the right sense of urgency.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy travel disruption works so well as a scam hook\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost scam messages fail because they arrive out of context. A random refund email from an airline you never use is easy to ignore. A refund message from the airline you are flying with next week, during a period of public travel disruption, is different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scammer’s job is to make you think: “This could be real.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat can happen in several ways. You might receive a fake cancellation email that links to a refund form. You might search for an airline’s customer service number and land on a fake support page. You might post publicly on social media asking an airline for help, then receive a reply from an account that looks official. Or you might get a WhatsApp message that knows your hotel name, travel dates or booking reference.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach version uses the same pressure point: you are worried about losing money, losing your flight or being stranded.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCriminals are already using disruption this way. In March, \u003ca href=\"https://www.theguardian.com/money/2026/mar/15/travel-scam-airline-accounts-fake-refunds-iran-war-flight-disruption\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eThe Guardian reported\u003c/a\u003e that fake airline support accounts were targeting travelers affected by Middle East flight disruption. Some victims were looking for help or refunds, then received replies from accounts pretending to represent airlines. The scam moved into direct messages, where victims were asked for details and sent links that led to money being taken instead of refunded.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe jet fuel story gives scammers another pretext. A message can claim that your flight was cancelled, your fare changed, your fuel surcharge failed, or your refund must be claimed within a few hours. None of those claims needs to be true to be effective.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe scam may start before the message arrives\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake travel message becomes much harder to spot when it includes real information.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the idea behind \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/reservation-hijack-scam\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eReservation Hijack scams\u003c/a\u003e, a form of targeted phishing where cybercriminals use real booking details to make a message look legitimate. In some cases, attackers may use information exposed through breaches or compromised travel systems. In other cases, they collect details from emails, social media posts, previous leaks or fake customer service interactions. Gen has previously described Reservation Hijack scams as scams that use real hotel booking details to make fraudulent payment or verification requests feel authentic.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat distinction matters. A scam message does not need your credit card number to fool you. Knowing your name, your destination, your travel dates and your booking platform may be enough.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat these scams can look like\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA travel disruption scam usually does not begin with a strange offer. It begins with something boring and administrative. That is part of the trick.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou may see messages like:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your flight has been cancelled due to fuel supply issues. Claim your refund here.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your rebooking is pending. Confirm passenger details within two hours.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Additional airport tax or fuel surcharge is required to keep your booking.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your hotel reservation will be cancelled unless payment is verified.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Due to high call volume, please continue this request on WhatsApp.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe exact wording changes, but the structure is familiar. There is a problem, a deadline and a link. The message may also move you away from the official channel into SMS, WhatsApp, Telegram, direct messages or a phone call. Once that happens, the scammer controls the conversation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is another detail worth watching: some messages do not ask for money immediately. They start by asking for your booking reference, last name, phone number or one-time password. That can still be dangerous. Those details can help scammers access a booking, impersonate you to customer support, reset an account or make the next message more convincing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eYour real passenger rights can be used against you\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an airline cancels your flight, you may have rights to a refund, re-routing or assistance, depending on where you are travelling and the rules that apply. The European Commission said passengers affected by cancellations continue to have rights to reimbursement, re-routing or return, airport assistance and compensation for last-minute cancellations in relevant circumstances. It also said airlines cannot retroactively add fuel surcharges to already published final ticket prices.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat information is useful for travelers, but it is also useful for scammers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake refund message becomes more believable when people already know refunds are possible. A fake rebooking link becomes more believable when airlines are genuinely changing schedules. A fake “fuel surcharge” demand becomes more dangerous when fuel prices are in the news, even though legitimate retroactive charges may not be allowed in many cases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why the safest route is not to judge the message only by whether the story sounds plausible. During travel disruption, many scam stories will sound plausible. Judge the channel instead.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eHow to check before you click\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the airline or booking platform’s official app or website. Do not use the link in the message. Open the app yourself, type the website address yourself, or use the contact details listed on your original booking confirmation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCheck whether the same cancellation, refund or payment request appears inside your account. If the message is real, the airline or hotel should be able to confirm it through an official channel.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe careful with social media support. If an account replies to your public complaint, check whether it is linked from the airline’s official website. Look at the handle carefully. Fake support accounts often copy logos and names, but have small differences in spelling, low follower counts or recently created profiles. The Guardian reported that fraudsters often scan social media for passengers trying to contact legitimate airline accounts and reply to them first, which makes the scam harder to spot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDo not share one-time passwords, payment card details or login credentials with anyone who contacts you unexpectedly. A real airline or hotel does not need your banking OTP to send you a refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe especially suspicious of pressure. “You have 30 minutes to claim your refund” is not customer service. It is a tactic.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you already clicked\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you entered payment details, contact your bank or card provider immediately. Ask whether the transaction can be blocked or reversed, and request a new card if needed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared login details for an airline, hotel or booking platform, change the password from the official site or app. Use a unique password, not one you use elsewhere. Turn on two-factor authentication where it is available.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared a one-time password or authentication code, assume the account may have been accessed. Check your booking details, payment methods and recent account activity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReport the scam to the airline, hotel or booking platform being impersonated. If the scam came through social media, report the account as impersonation. If money was taken, report it to the relevant fraud authority in your country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe real lesson for summer travel\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eTravel disruption changes how people behave. They search for support numbers in a hurry. They post publicly because customer service queues are long. They accept strange instructions because they are worried about losing a flight or refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers build around that behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest assumption is simple: if a message about your flight, hotel or refund creates urgency and gives you a link, pause. Go back to the official source. A real cancellation will still be there when you check through the airline or booking platform. A scammer needs you to act before you
